Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been acting way beyond how I normally act at work. I’m usually just a crew guy, you know, landscaping, groundskeeping stuff — I show up, do the job, go home. But lately I started trying to run like three different bids at once, calling city people, trying to line up plant suppliers, texting and messaging basically everybody I know about this park idea I got in my head.

And it wasn’t just like, “Oh, I had a thought.” It felt like I couldn’t shut it off. I’d be bouncing from one thing to the next — who to call, what materials we’d need, who might donate stuff, which city department handles what — and I kept feeling like if I didn’t do it right then, I’d lose the whole thing. It got kind of… bigger than me, I guess. So that’s why I’m here.

Um… no, I don’t think so. Not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing voices or anything like that. I mean, I was definitely very keyed in to stuff — like I’d notice a truck from a nursery drive by and suddenly think, “Oh, that’s a sign I should call them,” or I’d hear people talking near the job site and my brain would kind of jump to, like, “Maybe they’re talking about the park thing.” But that’s more me reading into stuff, I guess, not actually hearing something nobody else could hear.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s hard to say “euphoric” because I wasn’t walking around like everything was perfect, exactly, but I definitely felt way more fired up than normal. Like I had this feeling in my chest like I was on the edge of something big, and I was the only one really seeing how it could all come together.

I felt almost… bigger than myself, if that makes sense. Like normally I’m just trying to get through the workday, but then I was talking like I could make this whole thing happen if people would just get out of the way and move fast enough. It felt good at first, like exciting, like I finally had energy and purpose.

But it also wasn’t just happy. If somebody questioned the plan or told me to slow down, especially a coworker, I’d get pissed really fast. Like way too fast. Someone would say something normal like, “Maybe we should check with the supervisor first,” and I’d snap back like they were trying to ruin everything. Looking back, it was a lot stronger than the situation called for. So yeah, I guess I felt overly good and fired up, but also really touchy and irritable if anyone pushed back.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess there was a belief like that. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, though. That’s the thing. It felt completely obvious to me, like everyone else was just behind.

I honestly believed I was about to become the guy every town around here called for sustainable landscaping. Like not just getting a few more jobs or doing a nice project here and there — I mean I was thinking I could be the person people would call when they wanted parks or public spaces done the “right” way, more environmentally friendly, more thoughtful, all of that.

And saying it now, I can hear how big that sounds, because I’m still just trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of normal work. I don’t have some big company or a whole crew ready to go for every town. But at that time, I really believed it. If someone said, “That’s probably not realistic,” I’d think they just didn’t understand what I was seeing. It felt less like an idea and more like something that was definitely about to happen.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar II Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing voices or anything like that. I mean, I was definitely very keyed in to stuff — like I’d notice a truck from a nursery drive by and suddenly think, “Oh, that’s a sign I should call them,” or I’d hear people talking near the job site and my brain would kind of jump to, like, “Maybe they’re talking about the park thing.” But that’s more me reading into stuff, I guess, not actually hearing something nobody else could hear.\n\nI don’t remember anything where I’d look over and see something that disappeared, or hear someone say my name when nobody was around. If you mean like hallucinations, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or overly good compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s hard to say “euphoric” because I wasn’t walking around like everything was perfect, exactly, but I definitely felt way more fired up than normal. Like I had this feeling in my chest like I was on the edge of something big, and I was the only one really seeing how it could all come together.\n\nI felt almost… bigger than myself, if that makes sense. Like normally I’m just trying to get through the workday, but then I was talking like I could make this whole thing happen if people would just get out of the way and move fast enough. It felt good at first, like exciting, like I finally had energy and purpose.\n\nBut it also wasn’t just happy. If somebody questioned the plan or told me to slow down, especially a coworker, I’d get pissed really fast. Like way too fast. Looking back, it was a lot stronger than the situation called for. So yeah, I guess I felt overly good and fired up, but also really touchy and irritable if anyone pushed back.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you have a belief that other people said was not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there was a belief like that. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, though. That’s the thing. It felt completely obvious to me, like everyone else was just behind.\n\nI honestly believed I was about to become the guy every town around here called for sustainable landscaping. Like not just getting a few more jobs or doing a nice project here and there — I mean I was thinking I could be the person people would call when they wanted parks or public spaces done the “right” way, more environmentally friendly, more thoughtful, all of that.\n\nAnd saying it now, I can hear how big that sounds, because I’m still just trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of normal work. I don’t have some big company or a whole crew ready to go for every town. But at that time, I really believed it. If someone said, “That’s probably not realistic,” I’d think they just didn’t understand what I was seeing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… at the time, I was pretty certain. Like, not just “I hope this works out” or “maybe someday I could build a business around this.” It felt more definite than that. I really believed I was right on the edge of becoming that guy — the one every town around here would call when they wanted sustainable landscaping done properly.

And when people told me it wasn’t realistic, I didn’t really take that in. I’d hear them, but in my head I’d sort of dismiss it, like, “They just don’t get it yet,” or “They’re thinking too small.” It didn’t feel like they were giving me a reasonable reality check. It felt like they were missing something obvious that I could see.

Which is strange to say now, because I’m still just trying to keep my regular small jobs going and keep up with bills and make sure things are paid up. I’m not set up like some big operation that every town would be calling. But back then, yeah, I was convinced it was about to happen. Not in a vague dream way — more like it was practically already starting.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. Once I came back down to my normal self, it didn’t hold the same way.

I mean, I can still say I’d like to do good work, and I still care about sustainable landscaping and all that. That part is real. But that certainty — like I was about to become the guy every town around here called, like it was basically just waiting to happen — that faded when my mood and energy went back to normal.

Afterward, it was more like, “What was I thinking?” Not that the whole idea is stupid, but the size of it was just… way beyond where I actually am. I’m still trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of regular work. I don’t have the setup or the money or the crew to be that person for every town. When I’m normal, I can see that pretty clearly.

So yeah, during it I believed it like it was obvious. Afterward, it became more of an embarrassing, overblown idea than something I was still completely convinced was true.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it did. I mean, I tried to tell myself at the time that I was just “moving fast” or that I was finally getting things done, but looking back, it was starting to mess with work in a real way.

With landscaping, little details matter a lot — like measurements, materials, spacing, how much mulch or stone you actually need, what the grade is, whether something is safe where you put it. And during that time I started missing things I normally wouldn’t miss. I’d be putting together bids and realize later I’d skipped a measurement or guessed too quickly, or I’d write something down and then not double-check it the way I usually do.

That might not sound huge, but in my line of work it can turn into a big problem fast. If I underbid a job badly, I can lose money or look like I don’t know what I’m doing. If I get measurements wrong, the whole job can be off. And on a job site, if I miss something safety-related, someone could actually get hurt. That’s the part that bothers me the most.

So yeah, it caused problems. Maybe not like everything completely collapsed right then, but it was serious enough that I was worried I could lose clients or make one bad mistake that would cost me a lot — or worse, hurt somebody.

I mean, I was definitely very keyed in to stuff — like I’d notice a truck from a nursery drive by and suddenly think, “Oh, that’s a sign I should call them,” or I’d hear people talking near the job site and my brain would kind of jump to, like, “Maybe they’re talking about the park thing.” But that’s more me reading into stuff, I guess, not actually hearing something nobody else could hear.\n\nI don’t remember anything where I’d look over and see something that disappeared, or hear someone say my name when nobody was around. If you mean like hallucinations, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or overly good compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s hard to say “euphoric” because I wasn’t walking around like everything was perfect, exactly, but I definitely felt way more fired up than normal. Like I had this feeling in my chest like I was on the edge of something big, and I was the only one really seeing how it could all come together.\n\nI felt almost… bigger than myself, if that makes sense. Like normally I’m just trying to get through the workday, but then I was talking like I could make this whole thing happen if people would just get out of the way and move fast enough. It felt good at first, like exciting, like I finally had energy and purpose.\n\nBut it also wasn’t just happy. If somebody questioned the plan or told me to slow down, especially a coworker, I’d get pissed really fast. Like way too fast. Someone would say something normal like, “Maybe we should check with the supervisor first,” and I’d snap back like they were trying to ruin everything. Looking back, it was a lot stronger than the situation called for. So yeah, I guess I felt overly good and fired up, but also really touchy and irritable if anyone pushed back.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you have a belief that other people said was not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there was a belief like that. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, though. That’s the thing. It felt completely obvious to me, like everyone else was just behind.\n\nI honestly believed I was about to become the guy every town around here called for sustainable landscaping. Like not just getting a few more jobs or doing a nice project here and there — I mean I was thinking I could be the person people would call when they wanted parks or public spaces done the “right” way, more environmentally friendly, more thoughtful, all of that.\n\nAnd saying it now, I can hear how big that sounds, because I’m still just trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of normal work. I don’t have some big company or a whole crew ready to go for every town. But at that time, I really believed it. If someone said, “That’s probably not realistic,” I’d think they just didn’t understand what I was seeing. It felt less like an idea and more like something that was definitely about to happen.\n\nDoctor: At the time, were you completely certain that you really were about to become the person every town would call, even when other people told you it wasn’t realistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time, I was pretty certain. Like, not just “I hope this works out” or “maybe someday I could build a business around this.” It felt more definite than that. I really believed I was right on the edge of becoming that guy — the one every town around here would call when they wanted sustainable landscaping done properly.\n\nAnd when people told me it wasn’t realistic, I didn’t really take that in. I’d hear them, but in my head I’d sort of dismiss it, like, “They just don’t get it yet,” or “They’re thinking too small.” It didn’t feel like they were giving me a reasonable reality check. It felt like they were missing something obvious that I could see.\n\nWhich is strange to say now, because I’m still just trying to keep my regular small jobs going and keep up with bills and make sure things are paid up. I’m not set up like some big operation that every town would be calling. But back then, yeah, I was convinced it was about to happen. Not in a vague dream way — more like it was practically already starting.\n\nDoctor: Did that belief continue even after your mood and energy had gone back to normal?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I came back down to my normal self, it didn’t hold the same way.\n\nI mean, I can still say I’d like to do good work, and I still care about sustainable landscaping and all that. I’m still trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of regular work. I don’t have the setup or the money or the crew to be that person for every town. When I’m normal, I can see that pretty clearly.\n\nSo yeah, during it I believed it like it was obvious. Afterward, it became more of an embarrassing, overblown idea than something I was still completely convinced was true.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did your mood and energy cause serious problems at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I tried to tell myself at the time that I was just “moving fast” or that I was finally getting things done, but looking back, it was starting to mess with work in a real way.\n\nWith landscaping, little details matter a lot — like measurements, materials, spacing, how much mulch or stone you actually need, what the grade is, whether something is safe where you put it. And during that time I started missing things I normally wouldn’t miss. I’d be putting together bids and realize later I’d skipped a measurement or guessed too quickly, or I’d write something down and then not double-check it the way I usually do.\n\nThat might not sound huge, but in my line of work it can turn into a big problem fast. If I underbid a job badly, I can lose money or look like I don’t know what I’m doing. If I get measurements wrong, the whole job can be off. And on a job site, if I miss something safety-related, someone could actually get hurt. That’s the part that bothers me the most.\n\nSo yeah, it caused problems. Maybe not like everything completely collapsed right then, but it was serious enough that I was worried I could lose clients or make one bad mistake that would cost me a lot — or worse, hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Were you hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that the way you mean it. It was about two weeks, I think. Not just a day or two where I felt kind of motivated — it was a stretch.

It started around when I got really wrapped up in remaking myself for that park pitch, like trying to turn myself into this bigger, more impressive version of who I actually was. I’m still trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of regular work. I don’t have the setup or the money or the crew to be that person for every town. Before that two-week stretch? I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I’d call it a whole separate episode before that, exactly.

What I can say is that leading up to the crash, or before everything finally fell apart, I definitely had that feeling of being fired up — like I was bigger than myself somehow. Not just in a good mood, more like I had this extra force behind me and I thought I could push through anything.

And the other side of it was I got really touchy. If a coworker questioned one of my plans, or even just told me, “Hey, maybe slow down,” I would snap at him way harder than the situation called for. Like I’d take it as them disrespecting me or trying to hold me back, when maybe they were just being practical. At the time it felt justified, but looking back, my reaction was way out of proportion.

So… before that specific two weeks, I’m not sure I have another clear time to point to. But during the lead-up to me crashing, yeah, I was unusually fired up and irritable in a way that wasn’t really my normal self.

Like, not just “I hope this works out” or “maybe someday I could build a business around this.” It felt more definite than that. I really believed I was right on the edge of becoming that guy — the one every town around here would call when they wanted sustainable landscaping done properly.\n\nAnd when people told me it wasn’t realistic, I didn’t really take that in. I’d hear them, but in my head I’d sort of dismiss it, like, “They just don’t get it yet,” or “They’re thinking too small.” It didn’t feel like they were giving me a reasonable reality check. It felt like they were missing something obvious that I could see.\n\nWhich is strange to say now, because I’m still just trying to keep my regular small jobs going and keep up with bills and make sure things are paid up. I’m not set up like some big operation that every town would be calling. But back then, yeah, I was convinced it was about to happen. Not in a vague dream way — more like it was practically already starting.\n\nDoctor: Did that belief continue even after your mood and energy had gone back to normal?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I came back down to my normal self, it didn’t hold the same way.\n\nI mean, I can still say I’d like to do good work, and I still care about sustainable landscaping and all that. I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I’d call it a whole separate episode before that, exactly.\n\nWhat I can say is that leading up to the crash, or before everything finally fell apart, I definitely had that feeling of being fired up — like I was bigger than myself somehow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, before this most recent two-week thing, the time I keep coming back to is that period before I crashed. I don’t know if I’m separating it the right way, because it kind of all blurs together when I think about it, but I definitely wasn’t myself.

I remember feeling really fired up, like I was almost bigger than myself — like my ideas had more weight than everyone else’s and I could just force things to work if I pushed hard enough. It wasn’t just confidence, exactly. It felt like I had this engine in me.

And then if someone questioned me, especially a coworker, I’d take it really personally. Like if he said, “Maybe slow down,” or asked whether one of my plans made sense, I’d snap at him way harder than was reasonable. In my head at the time, it felt like he was trying to block me or make me look stupid, but looking back, I can see I was reacting like it was some huge insult when it really wasn’t.

So yes, there was another time where I felt unusually energized and irritable like that, before I crashed.

At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, though. That’s the thing. I don’t have some big company or a whole crew ready to go for every town. But at that time, I really believed it. Like, not just “I hope this works out” or “maybe someday I could build a business around this.” It felt more definite than that. I really believed I was right on the edge of becoming that guy — the one every town around here would call when they wanted sustainable landscaping done properly.\n\nAnd when people told me it wasn’t realistic, I didn’t really take that in. I’d hear them, but in my head I’d sort of dismiss it, like, “They just don’t get it yet,” or “They’re thinking too small.” It didn’t feel like they were giving me a reasonable reality check. It felt like they were missing something obvious that I could see.\n\nWhich is strange to say now, because I’m still just trying to keep my regular small jobs going and keep up with bills and make sure things are paid up. I’m not set up like some big operation that every town would be calling. But back then, yeah, I was convinced it was about to happen. Not in a vague dream way — more like it was practically already starting.\n\nDoctor: Did that belief continue even after your mood and energy had gone back to normal?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I came back down to my normal self, it didn’t hold the same way.\n\nI mean, I can still say I’d like to do good work, and I still care about sustainable landscaping and all that. That part is real. But that certainty — like I was about to become the guy every town around here called, like it was basically just waiting to happen — that faded when my mood and energy went back to normal.\n\nAfterward, it was more like, “What was I thinking?” Not that the whole idea is stupid, but the size of it was just… way beyond where I actually am. I’m still trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of regular work. I don’t have the setup or the money or the crew to be that person for every town. When I’m normal, I can see that pretty clearly.\n\nSo yeah, during it I believed it like it was obvious. If I underbid a job badly, I can lose money or look like I don’t know what I’m doing. If I get measurements wrong, the whole job can be off. Afterward, it became more of an embarrassing, overblown idea than something I was still completely convinced was true.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did your mood and energy cause serious problems at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I tried to tell myself at the time that I was just “moving fast” or that I was finally getting things done, but looking back, it was starting to mess with work in a real way.\n\nWith landscaping, little details matter a lot — like measurements, materials, spacing, how much mulch or stone you actually need, what the grade is, whether something is safe where you put it. And during that time I started missing things I normally wouldn’t miss. I’d be putting together bids and realize later I’d skipped a measurement or guessed too quickly, or I’d write something down and then not double-check it the way I usually do.\n\nThat might not sound huge, but in my line of work it can turn into a big problem fast. If I underbid a job badly, I can lose money or look like I don’t know what I’m doing. If I get measurements wrong, the whole job can be off. And on a job site, if I miss something safety-related, someone could actually get hurt. That’s the part that bothers me the most.\n\nSo yeah, it caused problems. Maybe not like everything completely collapsed right then, but it was serious enough that I was worried I could lose clients or make one bad mistake that would cost me a lot — or worse, hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Were you hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that the way you mean it. Do you mean actually being admitted and staying in the hospital, or just having things get bad enough that someone thought I should be checked out?\n\nI don’t really have a clear hospital story tied to that episode that I can point to. What I mostly remember is how out of hand my thinking and work decisions were getting, and how worried people were, but I’d want to be careful not to say more than I actually remember.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did this unusually energized, fired-up state last?\n\nPatient: It was about two weeks, I think. Not just a day or two where I felt kind of motivated — it was a stretch.\n\nIt started around when I got really wrapped up in remaking myself for that park pitch, like trying to turn myself into this bigger, more impressive version of who I actually was. From there it just kept going. I stayed in that fired-up, overconfident state for maybe fourteen days, give or take, before I finally kind of crashed back down.\n\nLooking back, it feels like it had a beginning and an end. It wasn’t my normal busy season energy. It was about two weeks of being convinced I was on this whole new track, and then it dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-week fired-up period ever started, had you ever had another episode where you felt unusually high, overly energized, or much more irritable than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Before that two-week stretch? Not just in a good mood, more like I had this extra force behind me and I thought I could push through anything.\n\nAnd the other side of it was I got really touchy. If a coworker questioned one of my plans, or even just told me, “Hey, maybe slow down,” I would snap at him way harder than the situation called for. Like I’d take it as them disrespecting me or trying to hold me back, when maybe they were just being practical. At the time it felt justified, but looking back, my reaction was way out of proportion.\n\nSo… before that specific two weeks, I’m not sure I have another clear time to point to. But during the lead-up to me crashing, yeah, I was unusually fired up and irritable in a way that wasn’t really my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent two-week period, was there ever another time in your life when you had a similar episode of feeling unusually energized or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this most recent two-week thing, the time I keep coming back to is that period before I crashed. Yeah, there was. Not like seeing people that weren’t there or hearing voices or anything like that. If you mean like hallucinations, then no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or overly good compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s hard to say “euphoric” because I wasn’t walking around like everything was perfect, exactly, but I definitely felt way more fired up than normal. Like I had this feeling in my chest like I was on the edge of something big, and I was the only one really seeing how it could all come together.\n\nI felt almost… bigger than myself, if that makes sense. Like normally I’m just trying to get through the workday, but then I was talking like I could make this whole thing happen if people would just get out of the way and move fast enough. It felt good at first, like exciting, like I finally had energy and purpose.\n\nBut it also wasn’t just happy. If somebody questioned the plan or told me to slow down, especially a coworker, I’d get pissed really fast. Like way too fast. Someone would say something normal like, “Maybe we should check with the supervisor first,” and I’d snap back like they were trying to ruin everything. Looking back, it was a lot stronger than the situation called for. So yeah, I guess I felt overly good and fired up, but also really touchy and irritable if anyone pushed back.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you have a belief that other people said was not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there was a belief like that. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, though. That’s the thing. It felt completely obvious to me, like everyone else was just behind.\n\nI honestly believed I was about to become the guy every town around here called for sustainable landscaping. Like not just getting a few more jobs or doing a nice project here and there — I mean I was thinking I could be the person people would call when they wanted parks or public spaces done the “right” way, more environmentally friendly, more thoughtful, all of that.\n\nAnd saying it now, I can hear how big that sounds, because I’m still just trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of normal work. I don’t have some big company or a whole crew ready to go for every town. But at that time, I really believed it. If someone said, “That’s probably not realistic,” I’d think they just didn’t understand what I was seeing. It felt less like an idea and more like something that was definitely about to happen.\n\nDoctor: At the time, were you completely certain that you really were about to become the person every town would call, even when other people told you it wasn’t realistic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time, I was pretty certain. Like, not just “I hope this works out” or “maybe someday I could build a business around this.” It felt more definite than that. I really believed I was right on the edge of becoming that guy — the one every town around here would call when they wanted sustainable landscaping done properly.\n\nAnd when people told me it wasn’t realistic, I didn’t really take that in. I’d hear them, but in my head I’d sort of dismiss it, like, “They just don’t get it yet,” or “They’re thinking too small.” It didn’t feel like they were giving me a reasonable reality check. It felt like they were missing something obvious that I could see.\n\nWhich is strange to say now, because I’m still just trying to keep my regular small jobs going and keep up with bills and make sure things are paid up. I’m not set up like some big operation that every town would be calling. But back then, yeah, I was convinced it was about to happen. Not in a vague dream way — more like it was practically already starting.\n\nDoctor: Did that belief continue even after your mood and energy had gone back to normal?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Once I came back down to my normal self, it didn’t hold the same way.\n\nI mean, I can still say I’d like to do good work, and I still care about sustainable landscaping and all that. That part is real. But that certainty — like I was about to become the guy every town around here called, like it was basically just waiting to happen — that faded when my mood and energy went back to normal.\n\nAfterward, it was more like, “What was I thinking?” Not that the whole idea is stupid, but the size of it was just… way beyond where I actually am. I’m still trying to keep my small jobs paid up and stay on top of regular work. I don’t have the setup or the money or the crew to be that person for every town. When I’m normal, I can see that pretty clearly.\n\nSo yeah, during it I believed it like it was obvious. Afterward, it became more of an embarrassing, overblown idea than something I was still completely convinced was true.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did your mood and energy cause serious problems at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, I tried to tell myself at the time that I was just “moving fast” or that I was finally getting things done, but looking back, it was starting to mess with work in a real way.\n\nWith landscaping, little details matter a lot — like measurements, materials, spacing, how much mulch or stone you actually need, what the grade is, whether something is safe where you put it. And during that time I started missing things I normally wouldn’t miss. I’d be putting together bids and realize later I’d skipped a measurement or guessed too quickly, or I’d write something down and then not double-check it the way I usually do.\n\nThat might not sound huge, but in my line of work it can turn into a big problem fast. If I underbid a job badly, I can lose money or look like I don’t know what I’m doing. If I get measurements wrong, the whole job can be off. And on a job site, if I miss something safety-related, someone could actually get hurt. That’s the part that bothers me the most.\n\nSo yeah, it caused problems. Maybe not like everything completely collapsed right then, but it was serious enough that I was worried I could lose clients or make one bad mistake that would cost me a lot — or worse, hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Were you hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that the way you mean it. Do you mean actually being admitted and staying in the hospital, or just having things get bad enough that someone thought I should be checked out?\n\nI don’t really have a clear hospital story tied to that episode that I can point to. What I mostly remember is how out of hand my thinking and work decisions were getting, and how worried people were, but I’d want to be careful not to say more than I actually remember.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did this unusually energized, fired-up state last?\n\nPatient: It was about two weeks, I think. Not just a day or two where I felt kind of motivated — it was a stretch.\n\nIt started around when I got really wrapped up in remaking myself for that park pitch, like trying to turn myself into this bigger, more impressive version of who I actually was. From there it just kept going. I stayed in that fired-up, overconfident state for maybe fourteen days, give or take, before I finally kind of crashed back down.\n\nLooking back, it feels like it had a beginning and an end. It wasn’t my normal busy season energy. It was about two weeks of being convinced I was on this whole new track, and then it dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-week fired-up period ever started, had you ever had another episode where you felt unusually high, overly energized, or much more irritable than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Before that two-week stretch? I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I’d call it a whole separate episode before that, exactly.\n\nWhat I can say is that leading up to the crash, or before everything finally fell apart, I definitely had that feeling of being fired up — like I was bigger than myself somehow. Not just in a good mood, more like I had this extra force behind me and I thought I could push through anything.\n\nAnd the other side of it was I got really touchy. If a coworker questioned one of my plans, or even just told me, “Hey, maybe slow down,” I would snap at him way harder than the situation called for. Like I’d take it as them disrespecting me or trying to hold me back, when maybe they were just being practical. At the time it felt justified, but looking back, my reaction was way out of proportion.\n\nSo… before that specific two weeks, I’m not sure I have another clear time to point to. But during the lead-up to me crashing, yeah, I was unusually fired up and irritable in a way that wasn’t really my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent two-week period, was there ever another time in your life when you had a similar episode of feeling unusually energized or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, before this most recent two-week thing, the time I keep coming back to is that period before I crashed. I don’t know if I’m separating it the right way, because it kind of all blurs together when I think about it, but I definitely wasn’t myself.\n\nI remember feeling really fired up, like I was almost bigger than myself — like my ideas had more weight than everyone else’s and I could just force things to work if I pushed hard enough. It wasn’t just confidence, exactly. It felt like I had this engine in me.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me, especially a coworker, I’d take it really personally. Like if he said, “Maybe slow down,” or asked whether one of my plans made sense, I’d snap at him way harder than was reasonable. In my head at the time, it felt like he was trying to block me or make me look stupid, but looking back, I can see I was reacting like it was some huge insult when it really wasn’t.\n\nSo yes, there was another time where I felt unusually energized and irritable like that, before I crashed.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent episode, had there ever been another separate period when you felt this same kind of unusually high or irritable energy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there was.
